			<div class="after-portlet after-portlet-lang"><span class="wb-langlinks-edit wb-langlinks-link"><a href="https://www.wikidata.org/wiki/Special:EntityPage/Q298664#sitelinks-wikipedia" title="Edit interlanguage links" class="wbc-editpage">Edit links</a></span></div>

<p><b>Robert Bellarmine</b>, <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">SJ</a> (<a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Roberto Francesco Romolo Bellarmino</i>; 4 October 1542 – 17 September 1621) was an Italian <a href="/wiki/Jesuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesuit">Jesuit</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholicism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal (Catholicism)">cardinal</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>. He was <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonized a saint</a> in 1930<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and named <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>, one of only 37. He was one of the most important figures in the <a href="/wiki/Counter-Reformation" title="Counter-Reformation">Counter-Reformation</a>.
</p><p>Bellarmine was a professor of theology and later <a href="/wiki/Rector_(academia)" title="Rector (academia)">rector</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_College" title="Roman College">Roman College</a>, and in 1602 became <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Capua" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Capua">Archbishop of Capua</a>. He supported the reform decrees of the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Trent" title="Council of Trent">Council of Trent</a>. He is also widely remembered for his role in the <a href="/wiki/Giordano_Bruno" title="Giordano Bruno">Giordano Bruno</a> affair,<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Galileo_affair" title="Galileo affair">Galileo affair</a>, and the trial of Friar <a href="/wiki/Fulgenzio_Manfredi" title="Fulgenzio Manfredi">Fulgenzio Manfredi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bellarmine was born in <a href="/wiki/Montepulciano" title="Montepulciano">Montepulciano</a>, the son of noble, albeit impoverished, parents, Vincenzo Bellarmino and his wife Cinzia Cervini, who was the sister of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Marcellus_II" title="Pope Marcellus II">Pope Marcellus II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-smith_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smith-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> As a boy he knew <a href="/wiki/Virgil" title="Virgil">Virgil</a> by heart and composed a number of poems in <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>. One of his hymns, on <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a>, is included in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Breviary" title="Roman Breviary">Roman Breviary</a>.
</p><p>He entered the Roman <a href="/wiki/Jesuits" title="Jesuits">Jesuit</a> novitiate in 1560, remaining in Rome for three years. He then went to a Jesuit house at <a href="/wiki/Mondov%C3%AC" title="Mondovì">Mondovì</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Piedmont_(Italy)" class="mw-redirect" title="Piedmont (Italy)">Piedmont</a>, where he learned <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>. While at Mondovì, he came to the attention of <a href="/wiki/Francis_Adorno" title="Francis Adorno">Francesco Adorno</a>, the local Jesuit provincial superior, who sent him to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Padua" title="University of Padua">University of Padua</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Career">Career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bellarmine's systematic studies of <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a> began at Padua in 1567 and 1568, where his teachers were adherents of <a href="/wiki/Thomism" title="Thomism">Thomism</a>. In 1569, he was sent to finish his studies at the <a href="/wiki/Old_University_of_Leuven" title="Old University of Leuven">University of Leuven</a> in <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Brabant" title="Duchy of Brabant">Brabant</a>. There he was ordained and obtained a reputation both as a professor and as a preacher. He was the first Jesuit to teach at the university, where the subject of his course was the <i><a href="/wiki/Summa_Theologica" title="Summa Theologica">Summa Theologica</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>. His residency in Leuven lasted seven years. In poor health, in 1576 he made a journey to Italy. Here he remained, commissioned by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XIII" title="Pope Gregory XIII">Pope Gregory XIII</a> to lecture on <a href="/wiki/Polemical_theology" class="mw-redirect" title="Polemical theology">polemical theology</a> in the new <a href="/wiki/Roman_College" title="Roman College">Roman College</a>, now known as the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Gregorian_University" title="Pontifical Gregorian University">Pontifical Gregorian University</a>. Later, he would promote the cause of the beatification of <a href="/wiki/Aloysius_Gonzaga" title="Aloysius Gonzaga">Aloysius Gonzaga</a>, who had been a student at the college during Bellarmine's tenure.<sup id="cite_ref-smith_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smith-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> His lectures were published under the title <i>De Controversias</i> in four large volumes.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="New_duties_after_1589">New duties after 1589</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: New duties after 1589">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below">
<a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Society_of_Jesus" title="Template:Society of Jesus"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Society_of_Jesus" title="Template talk:Society of Jesus"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Society_of_Jesus&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Until 1589, Bellarmine was occupied as professor of theology. After the murder in that year of <a href="/wiki/Henry_III_of_France" title="Henry III of France">Henry III of France</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_V" title="Pope Sixtus V">Pope Sixtus V</a> sent <a href="/wiki/Enrico_Caetani" title="Enrico Caetani">Enrico Caetani</a> as <a href="/wiki/Papal_legate" title="Papal legate">legate</a> to Paris<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> to negotiate with the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_League_(French)" title="Catholic League (French)">Catholic League of France</a>, and chose Bellarmine to accompany him as theologian.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He was in the city during its siege by <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Navarre" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry of Navarre">Henry of Navarre</a>.
</p><p>The next pope, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_VIII" title="Pope Clement VIII">Clement VIII</a>, said of him, "the Church of God had not his equal in learning".<sup id="cite_ref-smith_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smith-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Bellarmine was made rector of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_College" title="Roman College">Roman College</a> in 1592, examiner of <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishops</a> in 1598, and cardinal in 1599. Immediately after his appointment as Cardinal, Pope Clement made him a <a href="/wiki/Inquisition" title="Inquisition">Cardinal Inquisitor</a>, in which capacity he served as one of the judges at the trial of <a href="/wiki/Giordano_Bruno" title="Giordano Bruno">Giordano Bruno</a>, and concurred in the decision which condemned Bruno to be <a href="/wiki/Burned_at_the_stake" class="mw-redirect" title="Burned at the stake">burned at the stake</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Heretic" class="mw-redirect" title="Heretic">heretic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon the death of Pope Sixtus V in 1590, the Count of Olivares wrote to King Philip III of Spain, "Bellarmine ... would not do for a Pope, for he is mindful only of the interests of the Church and is unresponsive to the reasons of princes."<sup id="cite_ref-rice_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rice-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> In 1602 he was made <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Capua" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Capua">archbishop of Capua</a>. He had written against pluralism and non-residence of bishops within their <a href="/wiki/Diocese" title="Diocese">dioceses</a>. As bishop he put into effect the reforming decrees of the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Trent" title="Council of Trent">Council of Trent</a>. He received some votes in the 1605 <a href="/wiki/Papal_election" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal election">conclaves</a> which elected <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_XI" title="Pope Leo XI">Pope Leo XI</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_V" title="Pope Paul V">Pope Paul V</a>, and in 1621 when <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XV" title="Pope Gregory XV">Pope Gregory XV</a> was elected, but his being a Jesuit counted against him in the judgement of many of the cardinals.<sup id="cite_ref-smith_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smith-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Hobbes" title="Thomas Hobbes">Thomas Hobbes</a> saw Bellarmine in Rome at a service on All Saints Day (1 November) 1614 and, exempting him alone from a general castigation of cardinals, described him as "a little lean old man" who lived "more retired".<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Galileo_case">The Galileo case</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: The Galileo case">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Galileo_affair" title="Galileo affair">Galileo affair</a></div>
<p>In 1616, on the orders of Paul V, Bellarmine summoned <a href="/wiki/Galileo_Galilei" title="Galileo Galilei">Galileo</a>, notified him of a forthcoming <a href="/wiki/Decree" title="Decree">decree</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_of_the_Index" class="mw-redirect" title="Congregation of the Index">Congregation of the Index</a> condemning the <a href="/wiki/Nicolaus_Copernicus" title="Nicolaus Copernicus">Copernican</a> doctrine of the mobility of the Earth and the immobility of the Sun, and ordered him to abandon it.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Galileo agreed to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Galileo later complained of rumours to the effect that he had been forced to abjure and do penance, Bellarmine wrote out a certificate denying the rumours, stating that Galileo had merely been notified of the decree and informed that, as a consequence of it, the Copernican doctrine could not be "defended or held". Unlike the previously mentioned formal injunction <a href="#disputedinjunction">(see earlier footnote)</a>, this certificate would have allowed Galileo to continue using and teaching the mathematical content of Copernicus's theory as a purely theoretical device for predicting the apparent motions of the planets.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>According to some of his letters, Cardinal Bellarmine believed that a demonstration for heliocentrism could not be found because it would contradict the unanimous consent of the <a href="/wiki/Fathers_of_the_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Fathers of the Church">Fathers</a>' <a href="/wiki/Scriptural_exegesis" class="mw-redirect" title="Scriptural exegesis">scriptural exegesis</a>, to which the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Trent" title="Council of Trent">Council of Trent</a>, in 1546,<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Dogmatic_definition" class="mw-redirect" title="Dogmatic definition">defined</a> all Catholics must adhere. In other passages, Bellarmine argued that he did not support the heliocentric model for the lack of evidence of the time ("I will not believe that there is such a demonstration, until it is shown to me").<sup id="cite_ref-letter_to_Foscarini_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-letter_to_Foscarini-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bellarmine wrote to <a href="/wiki/Heliocentrism" title="Heliocentrism">heliocentrist</a> <a href="/wiki/Paolo_Antonio_Foscarini" title="Paolo Antonio Foscarini">Paolo Antonio Foscarini</a> in 1615:<sup id="cite_ref-letter_to_Foscarini_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-letter_to_Foscarini-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>The Council [of Trent] prohibits interpreting Scripture against the common consensus of the Holy Fathers; and if Your Paternity wants to read not only the Holy Fathers, but also the modern commentaries on <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Genesis" title="Book of Genesis">Genesis</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Psalm" class="mw-redirect" title="Psalm">Psalms</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastes" title="Ecclesiastes">Ecclesiastes</a>, and Joshua, you will find all agreeing in the literal interpretation that the sun is in heaven and turns around the earth with great speed, and that the earth is very far from heaven and sits motionless at the center of the world.</p></blockquote><p>and</p><blockquote><p>I say that if there were a true demonstration that the sun is at the center of the world and the earth in the third heaven, and that the sun does not circle the earth but the earth circles the sun, then one would have to proceed with great care in explaining the Scriptures that appear contrary, and say rather that we do not understand them, than that what is demonstrated is false. But I will not believe that there is such a demonstration, until it is shown me. Nor is it the same to demonstrate that by supposing the sun to be at the center and the earth in heaven one can <a href="/wiki/Saving_the_phenomena" class="mw-redirect" title="Saving the phenomena">save the appearances</a>, and to demonstrate that in truth the sun is at the center and the earth in heaven; for I believe the first demonstration may be available, but I have very great doubts about the second, and in case of doubt one must not abandon the Holy Scripture as interpreted by the Holy Fathers.</p></blockquote>
<p>In 1633, nearly twelve years after Bellarmine's death, Galileo was again called before the Inquisition in this matter. Galileo produced Bellarmine's certificate for his defense at the trial.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Duhem" title="Pierre Duhem">Pierre Duhem</a> and <a href="/wiki/Karl_Popper" title="Karl Popper">Karl Popper</a> “in one respect, at least, Bellarmine had shown himself a better scientist than Galileo by disallowing the possibility of a “strict proof” of the earth’s motion, on the grounds that an astronomical theory merely “saves the appearances” without necessarily revealing what “really happens.”<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Philosopher of science <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Kuhn" title="Thomas Kuhn">Thomas Kuhn</a>, in his book, <i>The Copernican Revolution</i>, after commenting on <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Cremonini_(philosopher)" title="Cesare Cremonini (philosopher)">Cesare Cremonini</a>, who refused to look through Galileo’s <a href="/wiki/Telescope" title="Telescope">telescope</a>, wrote:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Most of Galileo’s opponents behaved more rationally. Like Bellarmine, they agreed that the phenomena were in the sky but denied that they proved Galileo’s contentions. In this, of course, they were quite right. Though the telescope argued much, it proved nothing.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bellarmine retired to <a href="/wiki/Sant%27Andrea_degli_Scozzesi" title="Sant&#39;Andrea degli Scozzesi">Sant'Andrea degli Scozzesi</a>, the Jesuit college of Saint Andrew in Rome. He died on 17 September 1621, aged 78.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:208px;"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Roberto_Bellarmino.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/San_Roberto_Bellarmino.jpg/206px-San_Roberto_Bellarmino.jpg" decoding="async" width="206" height="271" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f4/San_Roberto_Bellarmino.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="395" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Roberto_Bellarmino.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>16th-century portrait of Saint Robert Bellarmine</div></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bellarmine's books bear the stamp of their period; the effort for literary elegance (so-called <i>"maraviglia"</i>) had given place to a desire to pile up as much material as possible, to embrace the whole field of human knowledge, and incorporate it into theology. His controversial works provoked many replies, and were studied for some decades after his death.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> At Leuven he made extensive studies in the <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a> and <a href="/wiki/Scholastic_theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Scholastic theologian">scholastic theologians</a>, which gave him the material for his book <i>De scriptoribus ecclesiasticis</i> (Rome, 1613). It was later revised and enlarged by <a href="/wiki/Sirmond" class="mw-redirect" title="Sirmond">Sirmond</a>, <a href="/wiki/Labbeus" class="mw-redirect" title="Labbeus">Labbeus</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Casimir_Oudin" title="Casimir Oudin">Casimir Oudin</a>. Bellarmine wrote the preface to the new <a href="/wiki/Sixto-Clementine_Vulgate" title="Sixto-Clementine Vulgate">Sixto-Clementine Vulgate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-smith_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smith-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Bellarmine also prepared for posterity his own commentary on each of the Psalms. An English translation from the Latin was published in 1866.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dogmatics">Dogmatics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Dogmatics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Disputationes" class="mw-redirect" title="Disputationes">Disputationes</a></div><p>
From his research grew <i><a href="/wiki/Disputationes_de_controversiis_christianae_fidei" class="mw-redirect" title="Disputationes de controversiis christianae fidei">Disputationes de controversiis christianae fidei</a></i> (also called <i>Controversiae</i>), first published at <a href="/wiki/Ingolstadt" title="Ingolstadt">Ingolstadt</a> in 1581&#8211;1593. This major work was the earliest attempt to systematize the various religious disputes between Catholics and Protestants. Bellarmine reviewed the issues<sup id="cite_ref-rice_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rice-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> and devoted eleven years to it while at the Roman College. In August 1590, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_V" title="Pope Sixtus V">Pope Sixtus V</a> decided to place the first volume of the <i>Disputationes</i> on the <a href="/wiki/Index_Librorum_Prohibitorum" title="Index Librorum Prohibitorum">Index</a> because Bellarmine argued in it that the Pope is not the temporal ruler of the whole world and that temporal rulers do not derive their authority to rule from God but from the <a href="/wiki/Consent_of_the_governed" title="Consent of the governed">consent of the governed</a>. However, Sixtus died before the revised Index was published, and the next Pope, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Urban_VII" title="Pope Urban VII">Urban VII</a>, removed the book from the Index during his brief twelve-day reign.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup></p><div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="324" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2266" data-file-height="3342" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dottrina_cristiana_tradotta_in_lingua_arabica.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Page of the short catechism of Bellarmine: <i>Dottrina cristiana breve</i>, 1752</div></div></div>
<p>In 1597-98 he published a <i><a href="/wiki/Catechism" title="Catechism">Catechism</a></i> in two versions (<a href="/w/index.php?title=A_Short_Christian_Doctrine&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="A Short Christian Doctrine (page does not exist)">short</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dottrina_cristiana_breve" class="extiw" title="it:Dottrina cristiana breve">it</a>&#93;</span> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=An_Ample_Declaration_of_the_Christian_Doctrine&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="An Ample Declaration of the Christian Doctrine (page does not exist)">full</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dichiarazione_pi%C3%B9_copiosa_della_dottrina_cristiana" class="extiw" title="it:Dichiarazione più copiosa della dottrina cristiana">it</a>&#93;</span>) which has been translated to 60 languages and was the official teaching of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> for centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Venetian_Interdict">Venetian Interdict</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Venetian Interdict">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Venetian_Interdict" title="Venetian Interdict">Venetian Interdict</a></div>
<p>Under <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_V" title="Pope Paul V">Pope Paul V</a> (reigned 1605&#8211;1621), a major conflict arose between <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Papacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Papacy">Papacy</a>. <a href="/wiki/Paolo_Sarpi" title="Paolo Sarpi">Paolo Sarpi</a>, as spokesman for the Republic of Venice, protested against the papal <a href="/wiki/Interdict_(Catholic_canon_law)" class="mw-redirect" title="Interdict (Catholic canon law)">interdict</a>, and reasserted the principles of the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Constance" title="Council of Constance">Council of Constance</a> and of the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Basel" class="mw-redirect" title="Council of Basel">Council of Basel</a>, denying the pope's authority in secular matters. Bellarmine wrote three rejoinders to the Venetian theologians, and may have warned Sarpi of an impending murderous attack, when in September 1607, an unfrocked friar and brigand by the name of Rotilio Orlandini planned to kill Sarpi for the sum of 8,000 crowns.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Orlandini's plot was discovered, and when he and his accomplices crossed from Papal into Venetian territory they were arrested.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Allegiance_oath_controversy_and_papal_authority">Allegiance oath controversy and papal authority</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Allegiance oath controversy and papal authority">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Oath_of_Allegiance_of_James_I_of_England" title="Oath of Allegiance of James I of England">Oath of Allegiance of James I of England</a></div>
<p>Bellarmine also became involved in controversy with King <a href="/wiki/James_I_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="James I of England">James I of England</a>. From a point of principle for <a href="/wiki/English_Catholics" class="mw-redirect" title="English Catholics">English Catholics</a>, this debate drew in figures from much of Western Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> It raised the profile of both protagonists, King James as a champion of his own restricted <a href="/wiki/Calvinist" class="mw-redirect" title="Calvinist">Calvinist</a> Protestantism, and Bellarmine for <a href="/wiki/Counter-Reformation" title="Counter-Reformation">Tridentine Catholicism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Devotional_works">Devotional works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Devotional works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During his retirement, he wrote several short books intended to help ordinary people in their spiritual life: <i>De ascensione mentis in Deum per scalas rerum creatorum opusculum</i> (<i>The Mind's Ascent to God by the Ladder of Created Things</i> 1614) which was translated into English as <i>Jacob's Ladder</i> (1638) without acknowledgement by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_Isaacson_(theologian)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Henry Isaacson (theologian) (page does not exist)">Henry Isaacson</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Art_of_Dying_Well" class="extiw" title="s:The Art of Dying Well">The Art of Dying Well</a></i> (1619) (in Latin, English translation under this title by <a href="/wiki/Edward_Coffin" title="Edward Coffin">Edward Coffin</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> and <i>The Seven Words on the Cross</i>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization_and_final_resting_place">Canonization and final resting place</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Canonization and final resting place">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bellarmine was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a> in 1930;<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> the following year he was declared a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>. His remains, in a cardinal's red robes, are displayed behind glass under a side altar in the <a href="/wiki/Sant%27Ignazio" class="mw-redirect" title="Sant&#39;Ignazio">Church of Saint Ignatius</a>, the chapel of the Roman College, next to the body of his student, <a href="/wiki/Aloysius_Gonzaga" title="Aloysius Gonzaga">Aloysius Gonzaga</a>, as he himself had wished. In the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> Saint Robert Bellarmine's <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is on 17 September, the day of his death; but some continue to use pre-1969 calendars, in which for 37 years his feast day was on 13 May. The <a href="/wiki/Ranking_of_liturgical_days_in_the_Roman_Rite" title="Ranking of liturgical days in the Roman Rite">rank</a> assigned to his feast has been "double" (1932&#8211;1959), "third-class feast" (1960&#8211;1968), and since the <a href="/wiki/Mysterii_Paschalis" title="Mysterii Paschalis">1969 revision</a> "<a href="/wiki/Memorial_(liturgy)" title="Memorial (liturgy)">memorial</a>".
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Bellarmine&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

Against William Barclay</i>; and <i>On the Primary Duty of the Supreme Pontiff</i>, are included in Bellarmine, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://oll.libertyfund.org/titles/on-temporal-and-spiritual-authority"><i>On Temporal and Spiritual Authority</i></a>, Stefania Tutino (ed.) trans., Indianapolis, IN: Liberty Fund, 2012</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<li id="cite_note-12"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-12">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFMartinich1999" class="citation book cs1">Martinich, A. Cambridge: Cambridge U.P. p.&#160;34.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Thomas+Hobbes%3A+a+Biography&amp;rft.place=Cambridge&amp;rft.pages=34&amp;rft.pub=Cambridge+U.P.&amp;rft.date=1999&amp;rft.aulast=Martinich&amp;rft.aufirst=A.+P.&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ARobert+Bellarmine" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-13"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-13">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Blackwell <a href="#Reference-Blackwell-1991">(1991, p. 126)</a>.<div id="disputedinjunction"> The Vatican archives contain an unsigned copy of a more strongly worded formal injunction purporting to have been served on Galileo shortly after Bellarmine's admonition, ordering him "not to hold, teach, or defend" the condemned doctrine "in any way whatever, either orally or in writing", and threatening him with imprisonment if he refused to obey.</div> However, whether this injunction was ever properly served on Galileo is a subject of much scholarly disagreement.<a href="#Reference-Blackwell-1991">(Blackwell, 1991, p. 127&#8211;128)</a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-14"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-14">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#Reference-Fantoli-2005">Fantoli (2005, p.119)</a>. Some scholars have suggested that Galileo's agreement was only obtained after some initial resistance. Otherwise, the formal injunction purporting to have been served on him during his meeting with Bellarmine <a href="#disputedinjunction">(see earlier footnote)</a> would have been contrary to the Pope's instructions <a href="#Reference-Fantoli-2005">(Fantoli. 2005, pp.121, 124)</a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-15"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-15">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Blackwell <a href="#Reference-Blackwell-1991">(1991, p.127)</a>. Maurice Finocchiaro's English translations of the purported <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20070930013053/http://astro.wcupa.edu/mgagne/ess362/resources/finocchiaro.html#specinj">formal injunction</a>, the <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20070930013053/http://astro.wcupa.edu/mgagne/ess362/resources/finocchiaro.html#indexdecree">decree</a> of the Congregation of the Index and Cardinal Bellarmine's <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20070930013053/http://astro.wcupa.edu/mgagne/ess362/resources/finocchiaro.html#certificate">certificate</a> are available on-line.</span>

<li id="cite_note-24"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-24">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.ecatholic2000.com/bell/psalms.shtml#_toc417747136">"A COMMENTARY ON THE BOOK OF PSALMS"</a>. <i>www.ecatholic2000.com</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. <li id="cite_note-25"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-25">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFBlackwell1991" class="citation book cs1">Blackwell, Richard J. (31 January 1991). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=v1gFDgAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PT123">"Chapter 2: Bellarmine's Views Before the Galileo Affair"</a>. <i>Galileo, Bellarmine, and the Bible</i>. University of Notre Dame Press. p.&#160;30. <a href="/wiki/Doi_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="Doi (identifier)">doi</a>:<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://doi.org/10.2307%2Fj.ctvpg847x">10.2307/j.ctvpg847x</a>. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-268-15893-4" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-268-15893-4"><bdi>978-0-268-15893-4</bdi></a>. <q>Bellarmine himself was not a stranger to theological condemnation. In August 1590 Pope Sixtus V decided to place the first volume of the <i>Controversies</i> on the <i>Index</i> because Bellarmine had argued that the pope is not the temporal ruler of the whole world and that temporal rulers do not derive their authority to rule from God through the pope but through the consent of the people governed. However Sixtus died before the revised <i>Index</i> was published, and the next pope, Urban VII, who reigned for only twelve days before his own death, removed Bellarmine's book from the list during that brief period. The times were precarious.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Chapter+2%3A+Bellarmine%27s+Views+Before+the+Galileo+Affair&amp;rft.btitle=Galileo%2C+Bellarmine%2C+and+the+Bible&amp;rft.pages=30&amp;rft.pub=University+of+Notre+Dame+Press&amp;rft.date=1991-01-31&amp;rft_id=info%3Adoi%2F10.2307%2Fj.ctvpg847x&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-268-15893-4&amp;rft.aulast=Blackwell&amp;rft.aufirst=Richard+J.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3Dv1gFDgAAQBAJ%26pg%3DPT123&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ARobert+Bellarmine" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

<p><b>Works of Bellarmine</b>
</p>
<ul><li><i><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.roger-pearse.com/weblog/2022/03/03/robert-bellarmine-opera-omnia-volumes-at-google-books/">Opera Omnia</a></i> - Google Books links to the 1870-74 reprint of his collected works in Latin.</li>
<li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://librivox.org/author/16210">Works by Robert Bellarmine</a> at <a href="/wiki/LibriVox" title="LibriVox">LibriVox</a> (public domain audiobooks) <img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Speaker_Icon.svg/15px-Speaker_Icon.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="15" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Speaker_Icon.svg/23px-Speaker_Icon.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Speaker_Icon.svg/30px-Speaker_Icon.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="500" /></li>

<li>Bellarmine, Robert (1614). <i><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://archive.org/details/bub_gb_YX6E6R32EpwC">A Shorte Catechisme of Card.<sup>all</sup> Bellarmine Illustrated with the Images</a>.</i> On Google Books.</li>

<li>Bellarmine, Robert (1753). <i><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.be/books?id=kbs-xzAKadgC&amp;pg=PA5&amp;lpg=PA5&amp;dq=#v=onepage&amp;q&amp;f=false">Vita ven.
